   - the git baseline `collect_git_status_porcelain` (19530, used at 8264);
   - `turn_start.record` (8285);
   - `HeldPages::of_turn` (8303).
6. The per-turn probe (3.2), then `confirm_lease_held` (4760) as the last step before the spawn, the way the Codex lane confirms right before it sends a turn (4756-4759).
7. Spawn and supervise (3.3-3.7).
8. Save (3.8), then return `JobExecution` (3011-3021).

### 3.2 Finding and probing `claude`

**Capture.** At RA/main.rs:85, right after `personal_browser::capture_and_scrub_process_capability()`, a new `claude_code::capture_and_scrub_launch_grant()` runs. That point comes before all of these:
- any thread;
- the skill-pack directory export (89);
- `arg0_dispatch`, which may load `$CODEX_HOME/.env` (95-99);
- the launch-credential scrub (103);
- skill-pack shims joining PATH (116-121);
- any job's `set_var` of granted secrets (RA/agent.rs:2284-2287).

The capture:
- reads and then removes `INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_BIN`, `INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_PATH`, `INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_USER_ID` and `INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_ENV_JSON` (the login-shell values D26 grants, as a JSON object), following RA/personal_browser.rs:76-110 for the read and the removal only. That function stops startup on a bad value (`bail!` at 88-90, returned through `?` at RA/main.rs:85). Here, a value that fails validation leaves the runtime running with a not-ready capability (`binary_missing` or `binary_unsafe`);
- validates them:
  - the binary path is absolute;
  - its canonical target is a regular file with an execute bit;
  - neither the target nor its directory can be written by anyone but its owner, apart from the `admin` group (below);
  - the target is owned by the effective uid or by root;
  - neither the path nor its target is under `WORKSPACE_DIR` or the project folder override (`project_workspace_dir`, RA/config.rs:641-651);
  - PATH entries are absolute, existing directories that are not under those folders;
  - a granted `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (a directory) and `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` (a regular file) are absolute, owned by the effective uid or by root, not group- or world-writable, and not under those folders (D26);
  - the user id is a UUID;
- snapshots the allowlisted launch environment (3.4);
- stores everything in a `OnceLock`.

The ownership and writability checks also cover the granted path's own directory, every symlink on the way to the target, and the PATH entries the child gets. A directory passes when only its owner (the effective uid or root) can write it, or when its only other writer is the `admin` group, whose members are root-equivalent through sudo: Homebrew makes `/opt/homebrew/bin` writable by `admin`, so without that allowance 4.1's own `/opt/homebrew/bin/claude`, and any PATH that holds it, would be refused. The same rule applies to the target checks above and to Desktop's static validation (4.1). A PATH entry that fails is dropped; a granted path or link that fails is `binary_unsafe`. The checks run again before each spawn, on whatever target the path resolves to at that moment, since Claude Code's updates move it (4.1), and the runtime spawns that checked target, not the symlink.

The runtime never looks `claude` up by name. It never copies, wraps, patches or writes the binary.

**Probe.**
- It runs `<bin> --version` and `<bin> --help` with:
  - the lane environment;
  - stdin set to null;
  - a fresh, empty, runtime-owned working directory, so no project settings can load;
  - a 10 s timeout and a 256 KiB output cap, with the process killed on timeout.
- Precedents: `node_on_path` (RA/skill_packs.rs:734-746) and rathole's `--help` probe (RA/config.rs:695-710).
- It runs at startup, for the capability, and again at the start of every lane turn. `--version` runs every time; `--help` runs again only when the version, or the binary's canonical path, size or mtime, has changed.
- At startup its three commands (`--version`, `--help` and `auth status`, below) share one deadline, one command's 10 s: the capability goes out with the runtime's first registration, and a granted `claude` that hangs must not hold up that registration, and every Codex turn behind it, for three timeouts. A startup probe that runs out of time gives `probe_failed`, an `auth status` cut short included, and the timed re-probe below corrects it, at the cost of one more registration. Each command of a per-turn probe keeps its own 10 s.
- It never sends a prompt, and Instafy reads no credential. The one sign-in check is Claude Code's own documented `claude auth status`, run with the same environment, working directory and limits as `--version`. It exits 0 when signed in and 1 when not, and its JSON `authMethod` is one of `none`, `claude.ai`, `oauth_token`, `api_key`, `api_key_helper` or `third_party`. The runtime keeps only the exit status and `authMethod`, and discards the rest of the output. It reports only whether this is a Claude plan sign-in, as the capability's `planSignIn` (2.10): true only when the rule below gives the plan line. A lane surface that has this result says "Runs on your own Claude plan" only when `authMethod` is `claude.ai` or `oauth_token` on v2.1.286 or later; earlier versions also reported a Console sign-in's stored API key as `claude.ai`. Otherwise, and on surfaces shown before any probe, such as the consent dialog, it says the turn runs under the person's own Claude Code sign-in. That sign-in may bill their API or cloud account, and with a cloud provider or gateway the traffic goes there instead of to Anthropic. The proxy-exception text in 7.1-7.3 says the same.

**What "supported" means:**
- The version, parsed with `^\d+\.\d+\.\d+`, is at least 2.1.325, the version Phase 0 ran on. The maintainer raised the floor from 2.1.314 to it on 2026-11-19, together with `CLAUDE_CODE_LANE_VERIFIED_THROUGH` (B.4). It includes `--permission-prompts` (2.1.259), the time limit on background commands in `-p` runs (2.1.285), and the 2.1.314 fixes: `claude -p` no longer exits 0 with no output when startup fails, and a settings file excluded by `--setting-sources` no longer supplies some settings.
- The floor is the evidence for the documented flags in 3.3. `--help` is read only for the bare check below. A flag missing from `--help` is logged, never a probe failure: Claude Code's CLI reference says `--help` does not list every flag, and an unknown flag already fails at startup, before the run.
- **The bare check passes.**
  - The lane is not ready (`bare_default`) when the `--bare` help line says bare *is* the default in print mode, even when `--help` also lists `--no-bare` (D-J). On 2.1.325, Phase 0 item 1 found a `--bare` entry that does not say so and no `--no-bare`, and the runtime's reading of that help agrees (B.2).
  - Wording that bare "will become" the default, or that refers to a future release, does not count.
  - When `--help` lists `--no-bare` and bare is not the default, the lane passes it.

**Capability.** A new `apply_claude_code_capability` runs after skill packs (RA/config.rs:315-321). Following 654-693, it always removes any launch-supplied value first.
- When ready: `"claudeCode": {"version": 1, "ready": true, "cli": "2.1.x", "verified": true, "planSignIn": true, "partialMessages": true}`, with `verified` as 2.10 defines it.
- When not ready: `{"version": 1, "ready": false, "reason": …}`. The reason is one of `binary_missing`, `binary_unsafe`, `probe_failed`, `unsupported_version` or `bare_default`.
- Absent when Desktop granted nothing, on any platform other than macOS in v1 (D18), and on a Personal Browser runtime.
- Never a path, which would reveal the OS user name.

The capability is fixed for each registration (it is sent at registration and renewal, RA/controller.rs:493-550). When a per-turn probe disagrees with it, runtime-agent registers again with the new value, so G6 refuses at send and Studio stops offering a lane that would fail at the runtime. While that value says not ready, runtime-agent probes again every 60 s and registers again when the result changes: no lane turn, and so no per-turn probe, runs to notice that the person has fixed Claude Code. Each registration keeps the value it sent, and the task that renews it compares that value with the published one when it starts and registers again at once when they differ, so a value published between a registration and the start of its renewal task reaches the controller at once, not at the next renewal. The per-turn probe is what decides: a result that is not ready fails the turn, with its reason, before anything starts.

**Reuse latch.** `claude_code::runtime_reuse_is_safe()` joins the check at RA/agent.rs:39-41. When cleanup is unconfirmed, the lease loop stops (1068-1071).

### 3.3 The exact command

```
<granted absolute path>
  --print
  --output-format stream-json
  --verbose
  --permission-mode acceptEdits
  --permission-prompts none
  --setting-sources user
  --strict-mcp-config
  --mcp-config <runtime-owned 0600 file containing {"mcpServers":{}}>
  --include-partial-messages
  [--no-bare]                            only when --help lists it
  [--resume <uuid>]                      only a session id the lane loader returned (2.7)
```

How the process is wired:
- It is started with no shell, one argv element per item.
- The working directory is the space's folder (`Config::project_workspace_dir`, RA/config.rs:644-651).
- stdin gets the prompt (3.5), then EOF.
- stdout and stderr are piped.
- The MCP config file lives in a runtime-owned directory outside every space folder, and is removed when the turn ends.

Why each flag:
- **`--print`, `stream-json`, `--verbose`, `--include-partial-messages`:** a headless run that emits machine-readable events, with the text deltas that stream the reply draft (3.7).
- **`acceptEdits`:**
  - File edits inside the folder are approved, and so are `mkdir`, `touch`, `rm`, `rmdir`, `mv`, `cp` and `sed` on paths inside it, so the turn can do coding work, including deleting and moving files that are then saved. Writes to protected paths such as `.git` and `.claude` still need approval, and are declined, with one exception: Claude Code's auto memory for the folder (below). A tool that writes such a path itself, rather than as an edit, may not ask: by a reading of 2.1.325's code, unconfirmed, a durable `CronCreate` writes the folder's `.claude/scheduled_tasks.json` without asking (1.2).
  - An explicit mode also overrides the Manual default of `-p` (version 2.1.302 and later), and overrides the person's own `defaultMode`, which might be `bypassPermissions`. That mode must never apply to a folder whose files come from teammates.
  - Edits outside the folder still need approval, and are declined, with two exceptions:
    - **Claude Code's auto memory for the folder.** Claude Code writes its notes for this folder under `~/.claude/projects/<slug>/memory/`, which every `init` names as `memory_paths.auto`, without approval: in acceptEdits, as Phase 0 ran it, and by a reading of 2.1.325's code in any permission mode. In Phase 0 a lane turn's two writes there succeeded with no denial, while the same flags declined writes to other folders outside the space and to the folder's `.claude/` (items 11 and 14, B.2). Claude Code documents those notes as loading in the person's later sessions in the folder, lane turns and their own terminal sessions alike (1.2).
    - **A directory the person's own settings add** (`permissions.additionalDirectories`). Claude Code's documentation says edits there follow the permission mode, so `acceptEdits` approves them as it does in the folder. Phase 0 item 11 therefore holds only when the person's settings add no directory: every `init` it saw reported `additional_directories: []`, and both declines of an outside write came from Claude Code's working-directory check. The runtime does not check this.
  - This bounds edits only. Bash commands that the person's own allow rules permit can write anywhere. Claude Code's permission settings, not runtime-agent's containment, bound its tools (7.1).
- **`--permission-prompts none`:** anything else that would need approval is declined. Nobody can answer mid-turn in this lane: runtime-agent has no channel that could carry Claude Code's permission prompts to the person. It runs Codex with no approvals (`AskForApproval::Never`, RA/codex.rs:2630-2631), fails `approval_required` jobs as not implemented (RA/agent.rs:1678-1696), and starts no `JobInputTask` for a lane job (2.8). The prompts a person does answer mid-turn, such as the Shared Browser's for its browser steps (packages/runtime-agent/assets/shared-browser-approval.js:33-40), belong to Instafy's own tools.
- **`--setting-sources user`:**
  - The person's own user settings, hooks, plugins and allow rules load.
  - Whatever the person's sign-in brings, if it is not tied to a settings file, loads too. Every signed-in Phase 0 run listed skills (`anthropic-skills:*` and a `schedule` skill), a web-fetch agent and the `RemoteTrigger` and `PushNotification` tools that the signed-out run, with an empty configuration folder, did not list, and the signed-out run listed the `WebFetch` tool, which no signed-in run listed (B.2). That run changed the sign-in and the configuration folder together, so that the account delivers them is an inference. They load in a lane turn, whose `init` lists them; no run compared that list with the person's own sessions (1.2, 7.4).
  - The space's `.claude/settings*.json` does not. Teammates write those files, and they can carry hooks, `env` (which could set `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`), `apiKeyHelper` and plugins.
  - Project memory stays out too. The project CLAUDE.md files (in the working directory and its parents), their imports and `.claude/rules/` load only through the `project` source, and `CLAUDE.local.md` only through `local`. So nothing Instafy seeds into the folder (step 3 in 3.1) reaches a lane turn as instructions. Phase 0 item 2 held on 2.1.325, with caveats, for `AGENTS.md` as well; its `claude-md-and-agents-md` variant, positive controls, files loaded on demand below the root and the floor version are still open (9, B.2, B.4).
- **Strict, empty MCP:** no MCP server loads. That covers:
  - `.mcp.json`;
  - the person's own MCP servers and plugin servers;
  - claude.ai connectors;
  - the Instafy plugin's `instafy mcp`, which would act as the person's CLI session (packages/instafy-cli/src/mcp.ts:14-22).

  Phase 0 item 3 confirmed this on 2.1.325 for a root `.mcp.json` and a claude.ai connector. Its setup had no MCP server of the person's own and no plugin server, so those two kinds are still to check (B.4). The launch check that fails a turn whose `init` lists any server (3.6) is the backstop meanwhile, for a server that `init` lists.
- **`--resume` without `--fork-session`:** `--resume` reuses the session id, so a resumed turn writes into the stored session, and the stored id changes only when a turn completes (2.7). A stopped turn's partial work therefore stays in a resumed session, and the next turn continues from it, as it would in the person's own terminal after an interrupt. A stopped first turn stores no id, so the next turn starts a new session.

**Never passed** (a test asserts the exact argv):
- `--bare` or `--dangerously-skip-permissions`;
- the `bypassPermissions`, `dontAsk` or `auto` modes;
- `--system-prompt`, `--append-system-prompt` or any variant of them;
- `--allowedTools` or `--disallowedTools`;
- `--add-dir`, `--settings` or `--model`;
- `--max-turns`, `--continue` or `--fork-session`.

The binary is never modified, wrapped, copied or bundled, and its sign-in methods are left alone.

### 3.4 The exact environment

The runtime calls `env_clear()`, then sets only the following values: allowlisted values from the startup snapshot, the values Desktop granted, a `PATH` built from values captured at startup, and one constant.

- **From the snapshot:** `HOME`, `USER`, `LOGNAME`, `SHELL`, `TMPDIR`, `LANG`, `LANGUAGE`, `LC_ALL`, `LC_CTYPE`, `TZ`, `SSL_CERT_FILE` and `SSL_CERT_DIR`.
  - This is `PERSISTENT_HELPER_BASE_ENV_KEYS` (RA/model_environment.rs:112-130) minus `PATH`, `TERM`, `COLORTERM`, `TMP` and `TEMP`.
  - `TMPDIR` was already sanitized at RA/main.rs:40.
- **From the grant (D26):** `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `NO_PROXY` and `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`, with the person's own login-shell values from `INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_ENV_JSON`, each only when Desktop granted it. A granted `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or CA file that fails the checks in 3.2 makes the lane not ready (`binary_unsafe`); it is never left out. Without the granted `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, Claude Code would load its default configuration directory, with its own sign-in, hooks and allow rules. Any other name in that object is ignored.
- **`PATH`, built from these sources, in order:**
  1. the directory of the granted path;
  2. Desktop's login-shell entries (`INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_PATH`);
  3. the launch `PATH` from the snapshot, taken before skill-pack shims are appended (RA/main.rs:116-121).

  The entries are deduplicated, absolute and existing, and never under the workspace root or the project folder. The native installer's target is a single binary that needs nothing on PATH to start. An npm `#!/usr/bin/env node` launcher needs `node`, and its target's directory, the package under `{prefix}/lib/node_modules`, holds none. npm links the command into `{prefix}/bin`, so entry 1 finds `node` when npm's prefix is node's own, as with nvm or Homebrew; with a custom npm prefix, or a granted link that points to that link, `node` comes only from entries 2 and 3.
- **One value Instafy sets:** `ENABLE_CLAUDEAI_MCP_SERVERS=false`.
  - It is documented by Claude Code and only makes it load less, namely claude.ai connectors.
  - It does not depend on `--strict-mcp-config`.

**Never passed:** any other variable. In particular:
- every `ANTHROPIC_*` and `CLAUDE_*` name, apart from a granted `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, which carries the person's own value;
- `OPENAI_*`, `CODEX_*`, `PROXY_*`, `CONTROLLER_*`, `RUNTIME_*`, `ORIGIN_*` and `INSTAFY_*` (including `INSTAFY_TURN_*`);
- the space, project and conversation ids;
- `ALL_PROXY`, and `HTTP(S)_PROXY` and `NO_PROXY` from anywhere but the grant;
- `NODE_OPTIONS`, and `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS` from anywhere but the grant;
- `DYLD_*`, `LD_*` and `TERM`;
- granted project secrets;
- everything in `INTERNAL_CREDENTIAL_ENV_KEYS` (RA/model_environment.rs:6-84).

**Why a snapshot.** By the time a lane turn runs, the live process environment may hold:
- granted secrets from other jobs (`set_var`, RA/agent.rs:2284-2287);
- the proxy key, as `OPENAI_API_KEY` and `CODEX_API_KEY`;
- `CONTROLLER_ACCESS_TOKEN` (RA/jobs/mod.rs:3799-3911).

It may also have lost `HOME`, if a job had a secret of that name, which is removed when the job ends (RA/agent.rs:2276-2282). And `arg0_dispatch` may load `$CODEX_HOME/.env` (RA/main.rs:95-99). The snapshot is taken before all of these.

**Authentication configured in Claude Code's own user settings still works. A sign-in kept only in shell variables, such as `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` or `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, never reaches it: that is the price of limit 5 (1.1).** A person who uses an API key or a gateway sets it in their own Claude Code user settings (`env`, `apiKeyHelper`), which load under `--setting-sources user`. Instafy neither supplies nor removes those settings. A configuration directory, network proxy or CA bundle set in the person's login shell reaches Claude Code through the grant above (D26).

### 3.5 The prompt

The prompt is exactly the UTF-8 bytes of `payload.prompt_text`, the person's recorded message, written to stdin and followed by EOF.
- **It is not in argv:** argv shows in `ps`, has length limits, and could be read as a flag. D30's fallback would put it in argv after `--` and accept that `ps` shows it; it is not built (below).
- **Nothing is added:** no history, memory, skills, context cards, attachments, browser cards, preamble, JSON schema or Instafy instructions. `build_turn_prompt` and `granted_turn_prompt` are never used.
- **No follow-up input from Instafy:** no steer, wrap-up, continuation or retry. Claude Code's own messaging socket can still bring words from another program of the same OS user into the session (1.2).

Claude Code loads its own memory. Of the CLAUDE.md files and rules, a lane turn under `--setting-sources user` loads only the person's own `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` and user rules: Claude Code's documentation says project CLAUDE.md files (in the folder and its parents), their imports and `.claude/rules/` load only through the `project` setting source, and CLAUDE.local.md only through `local`. So no Instafy-written text reaches a lane turn through the seeded CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md or INSTAFY.md, though Claude Code can still open those files as ordinary files. Phase 0 item 2 held on 2.1.325, with caveats, AGENTS.md included; what it still needs before phase 8 is in B.4. Claude Code also documents loading the person's auto memory for the folder, which it writes itself, whatever the setting sources, and Phase 0's token counts fit that (1.2, 3.3). Continuity comes from `--resume` and from Claude Code's own memory, never from Instafy.

`-p` with no prompt argument reads the prompt from stdin: Phase 0 item 4 held on 2.1.325, so D30's fallback, the prompt as the final argv element after `--`, is not built.

### 3.6 Supervision

**Spawn.**
- `tokio::process::Command` with `env_clear()`, the allowlist, `current_dir(folder)`, piped stdio and `kill_on_drop(true)`.
- No `process_group(0)`. `claude` stays in runtime-agent's own process group, which Electron created (DRA/index.ts:878-887) and which it signals and proves gone as a unit (DRA/index.ts:571-647). The tree proof comes before the controller is told the runtime stopped (DA/main.ts:1076-1078).
- That group holds `claude` and its own helpers, such as the git it runs, never its commands. Phase 0 item 9 showed that Claude Code's Bash tool runs every command, in the foreground or the background, in a shell that is a child of `claude` and leads a session and process group of its own. So Desktop's group stop and its proof never cover a command, and a group of `claude`'s own would not cover them either; it would only take `claude` out of Desktop's signals. The tracker below follows parent links whatever the session, so while runtime-agent lives it stops them, and Claude Code also stops a running background command on SIGTERM (bash-end, B.2). What happens after runtime-agent dies is in 1.2.
- On Windows the runtime's job object (RA/main.rs:27-32) would cover it, but v1 advertises no capability there.
- A `ProcessTree` (moved) observes descendants every 50 ms, as the isolated worker polls it (RA/isolated_tool_worker.rs:392, 408-409); `observe` (process_tree.rs:112-151) has no interval of its own. It keeps reparented ones it has already seen. The root is not a group leader, so `killpg` stays off (process_tree.rs:160-170) and descendants are killed one by one.
- What the polling costs (D-F). On macOS an observation reads only the tracked subtree, every 50 ms as before: the kernel's record of each tracked process (`sysctl`'s `KERN_PROC_PID`, which gives one for any user's process and for a zombie) and the list of its children (`proc_listchildpids`). Elsewhere it still reads every process on the computer through sysinfo, as Linux's isolated tool workers always have. The ignored test `process_tree::tests::what_observing_every_50_ms_costs` measures it (run by hand on a release build with `--ignored --nocapture`). Reading every process cost about 74% of one core on a debug build in phase 4, on a Mac with 770 processes. In phase 8 it cost 38% to 53% on a release build, on an M1 Max with 730 to 840 processes, about 92% of it system time, since the kernel answered for every process on every observation. The subtree read was measured on 2026-11-18 on that M1 Max with 710 to 760 processes, in turn with the old tracker over four one-minute runs each. It costs 0.3% to 0.6% of one core, 0.16 to 0.30 ms for each observation, where the old tracker cost 37% to 59%. Polling less often would also have cost less, but it would have widened the window in which a process that forks and exits leaves its child unseen. A record the tracker cannot read counts as a process that still runs, so a tree never confirms what it could not read. And with reads this fast, the stop path waits 25 ms after each round of SIGSTOP before it walks again, since a process stopped inside `fork` finishes the call first and its child appears only then (process_tree.rs:211-240).

**Bounds.**
- stdout lines are at most 8 MiB. A longer line fails the turn as `crashed`, as do more than 50 lines that do not parse.
- stderr keeps its last 16 KiB in a ring buffer, used only for classification and Details. The isolated worker keeps a bounded 16 KiB (RA/isolated_tool_worker.rs:368-380). The reader writes into a buffer the turn keeps, so when a process that escaped the tracker still holds stderr open and the reader is stopped after 1 s, the text read so far is kept. A tail cut short at its start leaves out its first line, and its end is redacted as a draft's is: an end that could be the start of the folder's or the home directory's path, a whole one included, is left out, because a reader stopped between two parts of a write, or a writer killed in the middle of a line, can cut the tail inside a path.
- The run log is bounded like the Codex run log (RA/jobs/mod.rs:135-149).
- From every line it keeps, whatever its type, the run log drops each key whose name contains `cost` or `credential`, in any letter case and at any depth, such as `total_cost_usd`, `costUSD`, `costBasis` and `credential_expires_at`, so a later version that adds one to another line loses it too. Of a `result` it also drops both per-model usage maps, `modelUsage` and `model_usage_since_last_result`, whole; of a `rate_limit_event` it keeps only `type`, `uuid`, `session_id` and, of `rate_limit_info`, `status` and `resetsAt`; and it leaves out what A.2 lists. On 2.1.325 a result carries a second API-price estimate and the sign-in's expiry, and a rate-limit line carries the plan's usage and overage state (Phase 0, B.2). The run log reaches every member who can read the chat, through the reply's metadata (2.6, 5.7).

**Redaction.**
- Every string the lane sends to the controller has `sk-ant-[A-Za-z0-9_-]+` replaced. That covers steps, drafts, the reply, failure text, the run log and the stopped-turn report, so Instafy never stores an `sk-ant-` value that Claude Code or a tool printed. That Claude's OAuth access, refresh and setup tokens also start with it is unverified (6).
- The absolute folder prefix becomes relative, and the home directory prefix becomes `~`, because both name the OS user. Claude Code also names its own per-folder directories with a slug of the folder's absolute path, which Phase 0 found in `init`'s `memory_paths.auto`, in a command that read the memory index and in a background command's output file (B.2), so the redactor also replaces the slug spellings of the folder and of the home directory. Command output, tool inputs and the run log can name paths outside the folder. So can the pre-flight's failure text (3.9), which quotes git's own words about a repository it could not read, and it goes through the same redaction.

**Stop.** A Stop arrives as the heartbeat's 409, which fires the cancel signal (RA/agent.rs:162-171), or as a refused message post (RA/agent_executor.rs:384-396).
1. SIGTERM goes to the `claude` pid only, never to the group, because the runtime is in that group. Claude Code documents exit code 143 for SIGTERM.
2. Wait up to 5 s.
3. `terminate_and_confirm` (process_tree.rs:175-282) sends SIGSTOP to every observed descendant and to the root, waits 25 ms for a fork already under way to finish, and walks again, until a walk finds nothing new. It then sends SIGKILL, and confirms they are gone within 3 s.
4. If that cannot be confirmed, the cleanup is marked unconfirmed, and the reuse latch stops leasing (3.2).
5. The turn returns the lost-lease error: there is no completion. The executor sends the stopped-turn report (RA/agent_executor.rs:276-282). The shared code that builds it changes for lane jobs. `TurnStart::attach_to_lost_lease` (RA/jobs/stopped_turn.rs:98-123) applies the lane filter (3.8). `StoppedTurnChanges::report` (286-332) names files by folder-relative paths, as the lane's own steps do, and stamps `provider: "claude-code"`. The controller matches the report's paths to the steps' paths as written (RC/agent/stopped_turn.rs:126-131, 208-215), so both must use the same form.

**Timeouts.**
- Wall clock: `INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_TURN_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`, default 2700, allowed range 60 to 3000. The save at the end of the turn (3.8, step 4) uses the job's internal workspace token. The controller mints it once, at lease, with a lifetime of max(`AGENT_TOKEN_TTL_SECONDS`, 3600) s, and nothing renews it (RC/agent.rs:2050-2054; RC/config.rs:1407-1411; RA/jobs/rolling_saves.rs:18-20). The cap leaves at least 600 s of that lifetime for the steps before the spawn and for the save; of those steps, the pre-flight stops at its deadline of 60 s (3.9). Longer turns wait for a workspace-token renewal (D11).
- Stall: 15 minutes with no stream event.

Either one uses the same kill path and fails the turn as `turn_timeout` or `stalled`. Nothing is saved.

**End of the turn.** A `claude -p` run can stay open after its `result`. It waits for a background command the main conversation started, such as a dev server, until the command exits or reaches its time limit: 10 minutes by default with a text prompt, up to 2 hours when Claude passes a `timeout`. It also waits for Monitor watches, pending `/loop` wakeups, and background subagents and workflows; by a reading of 2.1.325's code, a Monitor watch can run with no timeout, until it is stopped or the session ends. Every turn that background work starts opens with a `system/init` of its own and prints another `result`, though not every notification starts one (below). In `-p`, a subagent Claude spawns starts in the background unless Claude chooses to wait, so its outcome can arrive only in a later turn. Once nothing is left to run, `claude -p` exits by itself: the two Phase 0 runs with background work that were left to end on their own, `background-agent` and `bash-wait`, exited 0 after their follow-up turns (summary line 5 of each).

Phase 0 (item 5, B.2) showed on 2.1.325 that such a follow-up turn can run before the main turn's `result` is printed. In one run a background subagent finished just after the main turn's last model response, before the main turn printed its `result`: Claude Code ran the subagent's follow-up turn, with a second `init`, and only then printed the main turn's `result`, an interim sentence, and the follow-up's `result`, the subagent's answer, one after the other. By a reading of 2.1.325's code, `-p` holds a `result` back while a background subagent runs, and for up to 300 s while one has ended and its notification is not queued yet, and prints what it held back, in turn order, just before the next `result` it does not hold back. So a subagent still running when the main turn ends has its follow-up turn start before the person's `result` is printed. In another run, the main turn printed its `result` while a background command still ran, and the command's follow-up turn came after it. A follow-up's `result` carries an `origin` whose `kind` is `task-notification` and whose `runId` is the `run_id` its task started with; the `result` of the person's own turn carries none. The lane as first built ended at the first `result` that arrived with no task live, so in the first run it replied with the interim sentence, as a success, and the answer never reached the reply.

The adapter tracks the live background tasks from the `system/task_started`, `system/task_notification` and `system/background_tasks_changed` events, leaving out `ambient` ones. It also keeps each task whose `task_started` or task-list entry names a `run_id`, by that `run_id`, and counts the top-level `system/init` lines that passed the launch checks and the top-level `result` lines.
- A top-level `result` ends the turn only when all of these hold:
  - every top-level `init` read has its top-level `result`;
  - no live task is anything other than a background command or Monitor watch (`task_type` `local_bash`), for example a background subagent or workflow;
  - no task the turn started, its subagents' included, awaits its follow-up turn. A task that names a `run_id` awaits one, unless it is a background command, a Monitor watch or an `ambient` task, until a top-level `result` arrives whose `origin.kind` is `task-notification` and whose `origin.runId` is that `run_id`. A held turn's `result` that names the run does not end that wait; the next top-level `result` that is not held does (below). A task that does not say where it runs, or what it is, awaits one too. A task that says it runs in the foreground (`is_backgrounded: false`) never does, unless a later task list names it, which the adapter reads as running in the background: a foreground task's outcome is its tool result. Apart from a later event that marks a task `ambient`, or says it is a background command or runs in the foreground where earlier events said nothing, nothing else releases a task, so one whose notification Claude Code folded into the running turn stays awaited (below).

    A task one of the turn's subagents started awaits its follow-up turn as any other task does: the adapter does not tell the two apart. By a reading of 2.1.325's code, where such a task's notification goes under `-p` depends on what the task is. A background subagent or workflow notifies the subagent that started it only while that subagent's run is still running. Once that run has ended, whether it completed, even with commands of its own still running, failed or was stopped, the notification goes to the main conversation, as any task's does, and starts a follow-up turn that names the task's own run. A notification that went to the subagent starts no follow-up turn, and the follow-up wait below bounds that wait, as it does for one that reaches the subagent during its last model request, which 2.1.325 drops when the subagent ends with nothing keeping it alive. A background command or Monitor watch that a subagent started notifies that subagent alone, whatever its state, and the lane awaits neither. A subagent that ran in the foreground, failed or was stopped has its commands and watches stopped when it ends. When a background subagent's run ends while such a command or watch of its own still runs, the subagent has already notified the main conversation, in a report that 2.1.325 marks as possibly interim, and the command's or watch's notification then resumes it as a new run: a `task_started` with the same `task_id`, a new `run_id` and the same parent, whose end starts a follow-up turn that names that new run. The adapter awaits the new run as a task of its own once an event names it, but nothing holds the turn for it before then, since a live command or watch never does. So when nothing else holds the turn, it ends at the subagent's first follow-up, and the runtime's stop path ends the command or watch before the subagent resumes (B.4).

    A task that names no `run_id`, the shape of the fixtures written before Phase 0, holds the turn only while it is live. On a version whose task events name a `run_id` but whose follow-up `result`s carry no `origin`, nothing releases the wait: a task whose end was seen holds the turn until the follow-up wait below ends it, and one whose end was never seen until `claude` exits.
- **Notifications folded into the running turn.** By a reading of 2.1.325's code, the main conversation can also fold the notifications that wait for it into its running turn: when a tool step goes on to another model request, it takes them from its queue and adds them to that request, and a notification folded in starts no follow-up turn. Claude Code prints nothing of its own when it does, and the lines around it read the same when nothing was folded in. The lane does not try to detect a fold. Each sign it could read has a hole, by the same reading, and each hole would end the turn at the interim reply, before the answer:
  - a tool result does not show a fold: a tool step can end the turn instead, when a tool was deferred, a hook stopped the turn (the person's own hooks included) or a tool's result ended it, and such a step folds nothing in;
  - nor does the next model request: a worktree subagent's notification is queued only after its ended `task_notification` line is printed, behind the subagent's git work, so the request can start before it is queued; and the fold screens the notifications it takes, so one can stay in the queue while the request starts.

  So a task that ended without a follow-up turn stays awaited. If its notification was folded in after all, no follow-up turn comes, and the turn ends at the results read in one of two ways: `claude` exits by itself, as it does once nothing else runs, or the follow-up wait below runs out, while something else keeps `claude` alive, such as a background command or a Monitor watch. Phase 0 showed no fold.
- **Turns held for the next.** By a reading of 2.1.325's code, when the main conversation takes a task's notification to run while another notification it can be coalesced with is already queued behind it, `-p` runs the first as a held turn: one that adds the notification to the conversation, makes no model request and leaves the answer to the next turn. A subagent's, a background command's or a Monitor watch's end can be held, or be the notification behind a held one; a Monitor watch's event cannot. A held turn prints an `init` of its own, then a `result` of subtype `success` with `is_error` false, `num_turns` 0, an empty `result`, a null `stop_reason`, zero usage and the held notification's `origin`, a `task-notification` that names its run. The next turn is normally that of the notification queued behind it, under its own `origin`, and it answers both; it can be held in turn, so several held turns can come in a row. When no other message is queued, 2.1.325 runs a turn it owes the held notification instead, an empty prompt of its own whose `result` has no `origin`. Held `result`s print in turn order, as the others do, but can come after later turns' `init`s while `-p` holds `result`s back (above). A flag on Anthropic's server, on by default, controls the hold, and no setting or variable of the person's or the lane's turns it off, so the adapter expects it. No Phase 0 run printed a `result` of that shape.

  The adapter reads a top-level `result` as a held turn's when its `origin` is a `task-notification`, with or without a `runId`, its `num_turns` is the integer 0, its `result` is the empty string and it is no error. Such a `result` counts toward the first condition, the usage and the denials as any other, and never becomes the reply. The run it names is not followed up there, so a task that awaits its follow-up turn still does; the next top-level `result` that is not held follows up every run the held turns before it named, then reads its own `origin` as usual. That next `result` gives the reply when its own `origin` lets it, and also, whatever its `origin`, when a held `result`'s `origin` would have let that one: a background command's follow-up that answers a subagent's held notification gives the reply, and so does the turn owed to it. By the same reading, the turn that answers a held notification is normally the one queued behind it or the turn owed to it, but 2.1.325 can take another queued message first. The adapter takes the next `result` that is not held as the answer whatever started its turn, a peer message included. The turn owed to a held notification has no `origin`, so it reads as the person's own turn's and can give the reply, whichever notification it answers, a held background command's included. A peer message's report, a handback, can be held behind another the same way; only the report contract produces one, in a permission mode the lane does not use, and its `origin` is `peer`, which the adapter does not read as a held turn's. The end-of-turn check runs after a held `result` as after any other: a held subagent's run keeps the turn open, while a held background command's run, with nothing else awaited, does not, so the turn can end before the turn owed to it runs. During the follow-up wait, a held turn of a task the wait keeps comes just before the turn that answers it, so it restarts the grace with no restart used, even after the last one; each held turn is one notification's, so this cannot go on, and should nothing answer it, that grace runs out. A held turn of another task is a turn in between like any other. The `result` that answers a held turn follows up the held run, which is progress when the wait keeps that run.

  The shape is not a held turn's alone. By the same reading, a notice that repeats a usage limit a subagent hit prints the same line under the subagent's run, and so does a subagent's report pointer, which only a permission mode the lane does not use produces. So do the notices that a resumed session queues at its start about the previous session's background tasks that left no record of their end, and those name no run. The adapter reads all of them as held, and no turn answers them: a run one names stays awaited until the follow-up wait or `claude`'s own exit ends the turn. When Claude Code also reported a request it refused for the plan's limit, a `rate_limit_event` whose `status` is `rejected`, a held `result` that could have been the reply and that no later `result` answered fails the turn as `plan_limit`: the subagent behind it stopped at the limit, which the reply read does not say. A notice that names no run holds nothing. By the same reading, a resumed session runs those notices before the person's own turn, whenever the turn before left such a task, as one the runtime stopped at that turn's end, a dev server for instance, may leave. So nothing ends the turn, or starts the follow-up wait, before the person's own `result`, which has no `origin`, was read: the notices before it only delay the turn (B.4). A held turn that a `UserPromptSubmit` hook of the person's blocks prints a `result` with text instead, which reads as an ordinary follow-up of the held run.
- **The follow-up wait.** A notification can be folded in, as above, or go to the subagent that started its task, and then no follow-up turn comes. So once a top-level `result` was read, every top-level `init` has its `result`, nothing live holds the turn and every task that still awaits its follow-up turn has ended, the turn waits only for follow-up turns, and `claude` gets a grace of 15 s (`FOLLOW_UP_GRACE`) to start one. By a reading of 2.1.325's code, a follow-up turn's `init` comes only after Claude Code has run the person's own `UserPromptSubmit` hooks for it, refreshed its tools and rebuilt its system prompt. Phase 0's runs, whose record cannot show whether such a hook ran, saw a follow-up's first assistant line, a model request after its `init`, 1.3 s after its subagent ended in `background-agent` and 1.9 s after its command ended in `bash-wait`, by the streams' own timestamps (background-agent lines 34 and 45, line 34 being the subagent's last line before its notification; bash-wait lines 31 and 52, the command's end taken as its launch plus its 15 s sleep); 15 s leaves room for a slow hook. The wait runs only while `claude` stays alive with nothing else holding the turn.
  - A new top-level `init` takes the turn out of the wait, and the turn then reads on to that `init`'s `result`. Whenever the turn stops waiting only for follow-up turns and later waits only for them again, with no progress in between (below), the grace starts again, as a restart, and the time in between counts toward no grace. Another top-level turn does that, such as a Monitor watch's event turn, a background command's follow-up or a peer message's turn, and it needs the restart: by the same reading, Claude Code runs queued notifications one turn at a time, so an awaited follow-up queued behind another notification starts only after that one's turn.
  - One wait restarts its grace at most 5 times (`FOLLOW_UP_REARMS`), since a Monitor watch can keep its event turns coming as long as it runs. After the fifth restart, the next time the turn waits only for follow-up turns, it ends at once. One wait thus lasts at most six graces, 90 s, plus the turns in between; progress starts another, and the wall clock bounds them all.
  - A wait keeps the tasks that await their follow-up turns when its first grace starts, all of which have ended. Progress starts a fresh wait, with no restart used: one of those tasks no longer awaits its follow-up turn, as when a `result` follows it up. Nothing else is progress, the turn that answers a held one aside (above): not a held turn, a Monitor watch's event turn, a background command's start, end or follow-up, a peer message's turn, a task in the foreground or a follow-up that names a run the turn did not start, nor a task that a turn in between starts, its end or its follow-up. So turns in between that each start a task of their own still use up the restarts, two a task when it ends within its turn and is followed up; a held turn of a task the wait keeps uses none (above).
  - When the wait runs out, the turn ends at the results read, as at any end of turn: a follow-up that never came fails nothing, and the run log records `{"type": "instafy.ended_without_follow_up", "runs": N, "grace_ms": 15000, "restarts": R, "claude_exited": false}`, N being the tasks still awaited and R the restarts the wait used.
  - When `claude` exits by itself while the turn waits only for follow-up turns, with 0, or with 1 after a last top-level `result` that was an error, whichever turn's, the turn ends there as the wait would, at the results read, and the run-log line says `"claude_exited": true`. By a reading of 2.1.325's code, every turn `claude` ran has printed its `result` by then, held turns' included, and these two exits say nothing the `result`s do not: a `claude -p` with stream-json output that comes to its natural end exits 1 only when the last line it wrote that counts, from any turn, is an error `result`, such as a background command's follow-up that failed, and 0 otherwise. So the reply's `result` alone says whether the turn succeeded. Any other exit during the wait is judged by its exit status: the turn fails, as `crashed` unless something else classifies it, and the run log gets no such line. That covers 1 after a last `result` that was no error, 143 from a SIGTERM the runtime did not send, any other code and a signal. Exited at any other time, `claude` is judged by its exit status and the reply's `result` (below).
  - By the same reading, a crash of `claude`'s run loop, and an error that escapes a turn, first write an error `result` of their own, with no `origin`, so such an exit is 1 after an error `result`: the turn ends as the wait would, that `result` becomes the reply as the person's own turn's would, and the turn fails by it, with no code (5.5). Exit 1 after a `result` that was no error comes from a crash whose `result` could not be written, from repeated uncaught exceptions, or after a notice that repeats a usage limit (above), whose `result` reads as a held turn's while the exit follows the `result` before it. SIGTERM gives 143 only when it comes before `claude`'s own shutdown has started, SIGHUP gives 129, and SIGINT gives 0, even after the error `result` of the turn it interrupted; SIGKILL and the system's out-of-memory kill end `claude` by the signal. Desktop's stop signals the whole group, runtime-agent included, so a turn it stops ends as a shutdown (below). A `claude` that exits, or starts its own exit, before the runtime has read the `result` that ends the turn is judged by its exit status too, and that status can follow a later turn's error: such a turn fails though its reply succeeded.
  - No grace applies while a task that holds the turn is live, while an `init` read has no `result`, to a task whose end was never seen, or once `claude` has exited.
  - runtime-agent's supervisor asks the wait after every line it reads, so it sees each turn in between however `claude`'s output was split into reads, and on every 50 ms tick, so a grace runs out between lines too. The live suite's turn loop asks the same helper, `supervise::FollowUpWait`, the same way (9).
- Otherwise the adapter keeps reading, within the wall-clock and stall limits. A turn whose conditions never hold, such as one with an `init` whose `result` never comes, ends when `claude` exits by itself, and is judged by its exit status and the reply's `result` as below.
- When they hold, or the follow-up wait runs out, the runtime records the `result`s read and stops `claude` as Stop does (steps 1 to 4 above): SIGTERM, on which Claude Code terminates the process tree of any running Bash command, then up to 5 s, then `terminate_and_confirm`. It ignores anything printed afterwards.
- The reply, and whether the turn succeeded, come from the latest top-level `result` read that can be the reply: one whose `origin` is none, which is the person's own turn's, or a `task-notification` that names a run other than that of a background command or Monitor watch the turn or one of its subagents started. That covers the follow-up of any task the turn awaits, a subagent's or a workflow's, or that of a task a subagent started, whichever turn started the task, and that of a run the adapter does not keep. A background command's follow-up only says that the command ended, and a `result` with any other `origin`, such as the end of a turn that a message from another session started (`peer`, 1.2), is not the person's: neither becomes the reply, unless it answers a held turn (above). Nor, once any task event of the turn has named a `run_id`, does a `task-notification` that names no run, such as a Monitor watch's event turn: by a reading of 2.1.325's code, a Monitor event's notification carries no task id, so its `origin` names no run, while a task's follow-up names its own. While no task event has named one, the shape of the fixtures written before Phase 0, such a `result` can still be the reply. A held turn's `result` never is (above). The first condition counts every top-level `result` all the same, whatever its `origin`. Usage is summed over all the turn's top-level results, and their permission denials are combined (2.6, 3.7). Exit 143 after the runtime's own SIGTERM counts as exit 0. The `result` row in 3.7 and the success test in 3.8 follow this rule.

  The rule does not ask whose request a task serves, because the lane cannot reliably tell. By a reading of 2.1.325's code, a task can be named first during a later turn than the one that launched it, as when a subagent that its own command or watch resumes starts a new run (above); a task that an agent inside a workflow or a teammate launched carries no `parent_task_id`, which 2.1.325's own schema leaves out whenever the launcher has no task id of its own; and a background command's follow-up can go on with the person's request. A rule that let only the tasks of the person's own request give the reply would leave some of them out, and each one it left out would bring back the interim reply. So a helper that a turn in between started, such as a Monitor watch's event turn, a background command's follow-up or a peer message's turn, gives the reply when its follow-up comes last.

Phase 0 confirmed the task events' shapes on 2.1.325, with caveats: no line marked a task `ambient`, and a `task_notification` carries no `task_type`, so a task's type comes from its `task_started` or a task list. 2.1.325 also prints a `task_updated` line, which the adapter does not read; it always came beside an ended `task_notification`. No run showed a task a subagent started, a fold, a tool step that ended the turn, a held turn, a Monitor watch's event or a follow-up wait, so what the rule does with them rests on the reading of 2.1.325's code alone. Since newer versions run unconfirmed, the tracking still fails closed: a shape it does not expect keeps a turn open longer, within the limits.
- A `task_notification` removes a task only when it says the task ended. One with no status, or another status, changes nothing.
- A `background_tasks_changed` list adds the tasks it lists and removes those it marks ended or `ambient`. It never drops a task only because the list leaves it out. A listed task that is not a background command and has no id the adapter can read keeps the turn open until a later list no longer has such an entry.
- A task with no `task_type` keeps the turn open.

Apart from the limits, a Stop or a shutdown, a later `init` that fails the launch checks and output past the line bounds, a turn ends while a follow-up turn could still come in only three ways: `claude`'s own exit, which comes once it has nothing left to run unless it crashes; the follow-up wait, once every awaited task's end was seen and nothing else holds the turn; and the end-of-turn rule itself, when nothing awaits the turn still to come, as when a subagent's own command or watch has yet to resume it, or a held background command's notification has yet to get the turn owed to it (above). The wait ends one too early only when `claude` starts the follow-up turn later than the grace allows, as a `UserPromptSubmit` hook that takes longer would make it, or only after six other turns ran in between. Its answer then does not reach the reply, and the run log's `instafy.ended_without_follow_up` line shows that the wait ended the turn. Phase 0 showed neither case.

**Every exit.** After `claude` exits, by any route, `terminate_and_confirm` runs anyway, as the isolated worker does (RA/isolated_tool_worker.rs:470-479). Background shells and helpers that the tracker has already seen die with the turn. One whose parent exits between two observations escapes (1.2). If it stayed in runtime-agent's process group, it keeps running until Desktop stops the runtime, because `claude` shares that group and `killpg` stays off. If it started a group or session of its own, it outlives that too. A group of `claude`'s own would close only the first case, as the isolated worker's does (RA/isolated_tool_worker.rs:359; RA/isolated_tool_worker/process_tree.rs:102-105). Phase 0 item 9 showed that Claude Code's Bash tool produces the second: every command runs in a session of its own (Spawn, above), so `claude` keeps runtime-agent's group. A background command keeps its shell as its parent, so the tracker sees it; a command that puts a process in the background inside its own script and exits can still escape, which Phase 0 did not try. On a successful turn that left programs running, a status step says how many the runtime stopped: "Claude Code left N programs running when it finished; Instafy stopped them." A failed or stopped turn has no such step.

**Shutdown.**
- Electron signals the whole runtime group, `claude` included (DRA/index.ts:608-647).
- While the runtime's shutdown signal is set, the lane reports no completion. It still puts back what the turn changed under another chat's page first (3.8, step 2), a change on this computer only.
- Desktop's disposition then makes the controller fail the job as `claude_code_machine_stopped` (2.9).

**runtime-agent crash.**
- `claude` stays in the group. Desktop already covers this. When the root exits, the exit cleanup that `registerDesktopRuntime` installs (DA/main.ts:1544-1588) runs `stopAndDispositionDesktopRuntime`, which signals the still-alive group and proves it gone (DRA/index.ts:571-647) before the controller disposition. The turn's `ProcessTree` dies with runtime-agent, so a descendant that left the group survives (1.2), every command Claude Code started among them, unless `claude` stops it on Desktop's signals. No Desktop change is needed, and Codex's tool processes are covered the same way.
- The disposition fails the job as `claude_code_machine_stopped` (2.9). If the controller cannot be reached, the lease lapses, the job is never leased again, and the sweep fails it.

**Sleep.**
- Heartbeats stop, and the lease lapses (it lasts at least 30 s, RC/agent.rs:5130). A sleep that ends before the lease expires changes nothing.
- Once the lease has expired, the job is failed as `run_once_interrupted` (2.9): by the new step in the 10 s sweep, whatever the space's activity, or by the run-once sweep if the idle release requeued it first (RC/runtime/status.rs:1690-1717).
- If the sleep outlasted the runtime's idle TTL (300 s by default, RA/config.rs:389-392), the stale-runtime sweep also stops the runtime (RC/runtime/sweeps.rs:96-107, 2200-2242), because an expired lease is not an active job (RC/runtime/stop.rs:499-520). That stop fails the job as `claude_code_machine_stopped` (2.9) only if the sweep has not failed it yet.
- On wake, the next heartbeat gets a 409, because the heartbeat refuses to renew an expired lane lease (2.9), and the stop path runs.

**Launch checks.** These read every `init` event. Claude Code documents `session_id`, `cwd`, `model`, `permissionMode` and `mcp_servers` as always present on `system/init`, so a missing field fails the turn as `unexpected_configuration`, the same as a mismatch.
- `session_id` is required and must be a UUID.
- `cwd` must canonicalize to the folder in the first top-level `init`, the launch's own. A later `init`, a follow-up's or a peer message's turn's, must still carry `cwd`, but a `cd` may have moved it: by a reading of 2.1.325's code, `cwd` is the shell's current folder, which a `cd` inside the folder moves for the rest of the session, while `original_cwd` names the folder `claude` started in and does not follow the shell. So a later `init`'s `cwd` must canonicalize to the folder or to a folder inside it, both resolved first, so neither `..` nor a link that leads out passes. A `cwd` that no longer exists, such as a folder deleted after a `cd` into it, is read through its longest ancestor that does: that ancestor is resolved, the names after it are joined to it, and the path must then be inside the folder. Each of those names must be a plain name, not `..` (a `.` counts as no name at all), and the first must be missing from the ancestor altogether, so a link whose target is gone fails. Its `original_cwd`, when it has one that is not null, must also canonicalize to the folder itself. A `cwd` outside the folder fails even when `original_cwd` names the folder. A later `init` that fails this reads "Claude Code started a later turn in another folder than this space's."
- `permissionMode` must be `acceptEdits`.
- `mcp_servers` must be empty.

A mismatch terminates the turn as `unexpected_configuration`. These checks detect a flag that did not take effect. They cannot undo a hook that already ran: the flags are the prevention (3.3). Phase 0 found all five fields in every `init` on 2.1.325, a follow-up turn's second `init` included, which repeats the first apart from its `uuid` and passes the checks again (item 5, B.2). Every one also carried `original_cwd`, equal to its `cwd`, since no run moved the shell. Since 2026-11-19 they also read whether `init` names the space's own Claude Code configuration (D6). That means the output style the space defines or selects, or every skill, agent and command its root `.claude` folder defines, when it defines at least two: one name alone may be the person's own. Such an `init` fails the turn as `unexpected_configuration` too. A space's hooks run before `init`, so this stops a turn after them, not before. The checks read nothing else: not the tools, plugins, `additional_directories`, `memory_paths` or `messaging_socket_path` that 2.1.325's `init` also lists (1.2, 3.3).

### 3.7 Mapping stream-json onto existing messages

The adapter (RA/claude_code/events.rs) turns Claude Code's lines into the Codex-exec-shaped events that the existing consumers read. The streaming handler is today a closure inside `run_apply_job_observed` (RA/jobs/mod.rs:8367-8393, repeated for the retry at 9274-9298), which a lane job never reaches (3.1). It moves into a function that both turns call. The `/terminal` lane writes a command step's metadata (`codex_command_execution`) without Codex (RA/jobs/mod.rs:3425-3446). It builds that metadata directly, with no `event` key, rather than an event for `extract_codex_messages`, so it is a precedent for the command step's shape only.

| Claude Code stream-json | Synthesized event | Consumer |
|---|---|---|
| `system` / `init` | None. Records `session_id` and `model`, and runs the launch checks, a later one's by its `cwd` and the folder it started in. A top-level one also counts toward the end-of-turn rule (3.6). | lane |
| `system` / `task_started`, `task_notification`, `background_tasks_changed` | None. They update the live background tasks, and keep the tasks the turn started by their `run_id` (3.6). | lane |
| `system` / `permission_denied` | None. A permission denial, counted in the turn's denials (below). | lane |
| Top-level `stream_event` text delta (no `parent_tool_use_id`) | `item.updated` `agent_message` `{id: "<message id>:<block index>", text: <cumulative>, phase: "final_answer"}` | `ReplyDraftStream` (RA/jobs/reply_draft.rs:76-140), with plain text allowed |
| A text block that a `tool_use` follows in the same message | `item.completed` `agent_message` with `phase: "commentary"`, and a draft reset (beside the restart on `stream.retry` at reply_draft.rs:77-84) | status step (`extract_codex_messages`, RA/jobs/mod.rs:21514) |
| Bash or Monitor `tool_use`, then its `tool_result` | `item.started` and `item.completed` `command_execution` `{command, aggregated_output (≤ 4,000 chars), exit_code: null, status}` | command step (21578); label from `command_activity` (RA/jobs/activity_labels.rs:1510) |
| Edit, Write, NotebookEdit | `item.completed` `file_change` `{paths relative to the folder, status}` | file-change step (21628); `file_change_activity` (1543) |
| WebSearch `tool_use`, then its `tool_result` | `item.started` (when it has a query) and `item.completed` `web_search` `{query}` | search step (21797), "Searching the web" from its start; `web_search_activity` (1538) |
| TodoWrite, or where it is absent, TaskCreate and TaskUpdate | `item.updated` `todo_list` `{items: [{text: <content>, completed: <status is "completed">}]}`, the item shape the plan step and Studio read. For the task tools, the adapter keeps the list they build and sends all of it each time. With none of these tools on, no plan step appears. | plan step (21756) |
| Read, Grep, Glob, WebFetch, Agent (also Task, its name before v2.1.63) and any other tool | `mcp_tool_call` `{server: "claude-code", tool, engine: "claude-code"}` | tool step (21675). Its label comes from a new fixed table, `claude_code_tool_activity`: a quiet label for reads and searches, the web-read label for WebFetch, the generic tool label for the rest. `extract_codex_messages` uses that table only for an item with the `engine` mark, so a Codex turn's own MCP server named `claude-code` keeps the generic label. |
| Events with a `parent_tool_use_id` (Claude Code's subagents) | The same mapping, but never a draft | steps of the same turn |
| Thinking blocks | Ignored, and not logged | — |
| Top-level `result` | Nothing streamed. 3.6 says when one ends the turn, and which one gives the summary (its `result`). One whose `origin` is a `task-notification`, with `num_turns` 0, an empty `result` and no error, ends a held turn: it never gives the summary, and the run it names is followed up at the next `result` that is not held (3.6). The `claude-code/usage` artifact sums the usage of every top-level `result` read. | completion |
| Anything else | Ignored, but kept in the run log, less what 3.6 and A.2 leave out of it | — |

Rules that apply to every row:
- **Labels** come only from the runtime's own tables (packages/runtime-contracts/src/chat_activity.rs:1-13). New labels go into `chat_activity_fixtures.json`, so the runtime, the controller and Studio all check them.
- **Step metadata stores the synthesized event**, for example `"event": event.clone()` at RA/jobs/mod.rs:21666. Events therefore carry only relative paths, bounded output and redacted strings. A path outside the folder shows as "outside this space".
- **The reply** is the `result` of the top-level `result` that 3.6 takes the reply from: the latest one read that 3.6 lets be the reply, such as the person's own turn's or a subagent's follow-up's. It is redacted, with the folder prefix made relative. Instafy appends nothing to it. The notes that a Codex turn appends to its reply (held pages and not-saved files, RA/jobs/mod.rs:10505-10506) become separate status steps instead.
- **Notes.** Every status step the turn sends as a note of its own carries `"presentation": {"placement": "reply_note"}` in its metadata: what stayed on this computer and what its save left out (3.8), a save that failed after a good run (D-C/D-V), permission denials and programs stopped (3.6). The controller keeps a runtime's step metadata under `details`, so the hint arrives as `details.presentation.placement`, and Studio shows such notes as plain lines under the reply (5.7). The hint is generic: Studio's core reads it, never the lane's `claude_code_*` kinds.
- **Permission denials** come from what Claude Code reports: the `permission_denials` of every top-level `result` read, since by a reading of 2.1.325's code each lists only its own result's (Phase 0 item 5, B.2), and its `system/permission_denied` lines, combined so that a tool call counts once. When no `result` carries `permission_denials` and no such line came, they come from tool results that report a denial. They produce a note, "Claude Code skipped N steps that need your OK…", whose metadata keeps the tool names (`tools`); Studio shows the sentence. The turn still succeeds.

### 3.8 Saving files

Only a successful run saves. Success means exit 0 and a `result` whose `is_error` is not true, both read as the end-of-turn rule in 3.6 says: exit 143 after the runtime's own SIGTERM counts as 0, and when `claude` exits by itself during the follow-up wait with 0, or with 1 after a last `result` that was an error, the `result` alone decides; any other exit during that wait fails the turn. A held turn's `result` that no later `result` answered, after Claude Code refused a request for the plan's limit, fails the turn as `plan_limit` (3.6). This matches the Codex lane (RA/jobs/mod.rs:10374-10462).

1. `checkpoint_files_with_status_delta(&folder, before, &[])` (19720-19734). It already drops reserved and unpublishable paths (19750-19757). With no reported files, it returns an empty list when the baseline is missing or the final `git status` fails or hits its 10 s timeout (19725-19731; RA/jobs/workspace_change_detection.rs:19). So a turn whose baseline could not be read (3.1, step 5) ends before spawning, as a pre-turn refresh that refuses saves does (3.1, step 3), and a folder with no repository never gets this far: the pre-flight refuses it (3.9, D-A). When only the final read fails, the lane adds a note saying the turn's changes stayed on this computer and were not saved, rather than ending as if nothing changed: "Claude Code's changes stayed on this computer and weren't saved to the space: Instafy couldn't read what changed in this space's folder." (kind `claude_code_changes_not_saved`, reason `status_unreadable`). To tell that case from a turn that changed nothing, the lane reads the final status itself with the same two pieces, `collect_git_status_porcelain` and `status_delta_files_for_checkpoint`.
2. `held_pages.leave_out_held` (10384). Before any of this, once `claude` has exited and whether or not the run succeeded, a turn the runtime's shutdown ended included, `held_pages.put_back(&folder, before, TurnSteps::of_events(&folder, &events))` puts back what the turn changed under another chat's page, as the Codex turn does (8484-8498). `held_pages.artifact()` (10492) joins the artifacts.
3. **The lane filter.** Drop every path with a `.claude` segment, every `CLAUDE.local.md` and the `.mcp.json` at the folder's root, all compared as a Mac's folder compares names: ignoring case, as `is_reserved_path` does because macOS folders are case-insensitive (packages/origin-http-server/src/paths.rs:4-6), and beyond ASCII too, since APFS also opens `.mcp.jſon` (with a long s) as `.mcp.json`. The comparison is Unicode's canonical caseless match: NFD, then default case folding, then NFD again. The first two are Claude Code's per-person files, and a `.claude/settings.json` saved into the space would carry hooks onto teammates' machines. A saved root `.mcp.json` would offer them MCP servers to run and, like a saved `.claude/` path, would make the pre-flight (3.9) refuse every later lane turn in the space. Also drop every changed file whose name or content holds a token: `sk-ant-`, ignoring ASCII case, followed by at least 20 of `[A-Za-z0-9_-]` and not preceded by a letter or digit. A name is checked before the content, so a deleted file whose name holds one stays off the save too, and its path never reaches `apply/files`, `origin/apply` or a stopped turn's report. Name each such file in the not-saved status step, with the token replaced. The publish policy matches file names only (packages/origin-http-server/src/publish_policy.rs:108-129), so without this a token that Claude Code or a tool wrote into an ordinary file would be stored in the space and reach every teammate's checkout. A bare mention of the prefix does not count, so code and documents that only name it still save, as this repository's do (RA/jobs/card_text.rs:1153, RA/jobs/skill_declaration.rs:845).
4. `commit_to_hosted_origin`, with the verified workspace token (10404-10419). **A save that fails after a successful run fails nothing (D-C/D-V).** The reply is delivered with the note "Claude Code's changes stayed on this computer and weren't saved to the space." (kind `claude_code_changes_not_saved`, reason `save_failed`, with the files' paths, tokens replaced). The redacted error, with its causes, goes in an `origin/apply-error` artifact. `apply/files` then lists nothing, since nothing was saved, so Studio's files card and Undo offer nothing. A Stop that refused the save still ends as a stopped turn, as today.
5. `preview_pictures::reload_and_picture_turn_sites` (10464-10472). There are no page cards in v1, because `page_cards::post_missing_cards` (10474-10482) reads Codex events.
6. Status steps that list what stayed on this computer and what was not saved. Like every note of the turn, they carry the `reply_note` placement (3.7).

`JobExecution` is returned with:
- `provider: "claude-code"`;
- `messages_streamed`, set as the Codex turn sets it: true only if every streamed step reached the controller (RA/jobs/mod.rs:8586-8587). Otherwise the executor posts the steps again (RA/agent.rs:1759-1779);
- the lane conversation state;
- the artifacts `apply/files` (the saved files with folder-relative paths, built as `build_codex_artifacts` builds it, RA/jobs/mod.rs:21202-21223, with `metadata.provider: "claude-code"`), `origin/apply`, `claude-code/usage` and a bounded `claude-code/run-log`. Never a `codex/run-log`. Studio's file list and Undo read `apply/files`, and so does the stopped-turn report when a Stop refuses the completion (RA/agent.rs:1825-1835).

A failure or timeout saves nothing; its changes stay in the folder, as after a failed Codex turn. A Stop sends the stopped-turn report. The lane filter applies to lane saves only (D21). git-service's deny list stays reserved for build output (packages/git-service/src/policy.rs:20-42).

### 3.9 Refusals before spawning

`LaneJob::of` runs in the pre-run refusal (3.1) and again when the turn starts. It covers every check below that needs only the job and the captured grant. The workspace token's verification, the folder pre-flight and the per-turn probe run inside the turn instead (3.1, steps 2, 4 and 6): the pre-flight needs the refreshed checkout, and the token and the probe are checked where the turn uses them. The job fails without starting `claude` unless every one of these holds:

- **Grant and probe:** the grant was captured, and the per-turn probe says ready.
- **Job shape:**
  - `engine == "claude-code"` and `lease_attempts == 1`;
  - `proxy`, `controller_token`, `grants`, `grant_token` and `credential_id` are absent;
  - `workspace_token` is present and verifies.
- **Person and mode:**
  - `payload.user_id` equals the granted `INSTAFY_CLAUDE_CODE_USER_ID`;
  - `payload.metadata.input.kind == "person"`;
  - `execution_mode(&job)` is Apply (RA/agent.rs:1871-1882): `metadata.execution_mode`, and `metadata.executionMode`, which the lane's payload allowlist never carries, are absent or `apply`. The payload's top-level `executionMode` is the workspace mode, `shared_write` (2.5), and is not read.
- **Markers:** none of these is present:
  - `job_is_unattended` or `job_is_an_automations_run` (RA/jobs/mod.rs:4115-4140);
  - plan or worker markers, or the evaluation marker;
  - `plan_seed`, hand-back keys, `retryOfMessageId`, `goalContinuation` or `goal_continuation` (both spellings, as RC/job_trigger.rs:22 reads them);
  - a browser transport, `dispatchContent` or attachments.
- **Prompt:** non-empty, at most `CLAUDE_CODE_LANE_MAX_PROMPT_CHARS` (new, the same value G5 enforces, since dispatch has no prompt limit today), and not starting with `/` once leading whitespace is trimmed, as the runtime reads its own slash commands (RA/jobs/mod.rs:3124, RA/jobs/git_sync.rs:20).
- **The folder pre-flight**, run on the checkout after the pre-turn refresh. Its git commands read `.instafy/.git`, the repository the origin saves to, with the folder as work tree whenever that exists (`canonical_git_args`, RA/jobs/mod.rs:5319-5332; packages/origin-http-server/src/git.rs:634-646), otherwise the folder's `.git`. `workspace_git_args` reads a top-level `.git` first (RA/jobs/mod.rs:5315-5317, 19484-19491), which in a folder that has both is not the repository the origin saves to. Each command runs as `workspace_git_command` runs git, with hooks and fsmonitor off (19501-19515). A git command that fails refuses the turn as `preflight_failed`. A folder with neither repository (a Desktop folder with no `.git` of its own, when the controller advertises no git remote, docs/Architecture.md:41) is refused at the pre-flight's start, before it reads anything, as `preflight_failed` with the first line "This space's folder on this computer has no git repository, so Instafy couldn't tell what a turn changes." (D-A): nothing there records what a turn changes, so no turn could save or say what it changed.
  - **A space's own Claude Code configuration is allowed (D6).**
    - Since 2026-11-19 the pre-flight no longer refuses tracked `.claude` paths, or a `.claude` entry or `.mcp.json` at the root. The flags keep them out of the turn, and the launch checks stop a turn whose `init` shows them loaded (3.6).
    - This also ends the refusals of `.claude/` files that the person's own sessions or a tool make: `.claude/settings.local.json`, Claude Code's worktrees, or a durable `CronCreate`'s `.claude/scheduled_tasks.json`.
    - The lane filter still keeps `.claude/` out of every save (3.8).
    - The import check below matches `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md` and `AGENTS.md` as a Mac's folder matches names, as the lane filter does. Claude Code opens a root `AGENTſ.md` (with a long s) as `AGENTS.md`, so the root's memory files are looked up through the folder itself, which opens a name as Claude Code will, and named by the entry the folder holds.
  - **Imports (`unsafe_import`).** Check every tracked `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md` and `AGENTS.md`, those at the folder's root whether tracked or not, and every file they import, followed recursively with a bound. From v2.1.277 Claude Code reads `AGENTS.md` as project instructions, at the root and in subdirectories, and expands its `@` imports. No path that a word after an `@` could import, anywhere in such a file, code, HTML and comments included (below), may be in the home folder (`~/…`), be absolute below an entry the filesystem's root holds, lead out of the folder to anything but a folder above it, resolve outside the folder after following symlinks, or resolve, after following symlinks, to an existing file that git does not track. An untracked or ignored file such as `.env` exists only on this computer, because the publish policy never saves it (packages/origin-http-server/src/publish_policy.rs:110-123). So a teammate's `@.env`, or an import of a tracked symlink that points at it, would put the person's own secrets in front of a turn whose reply everyone reads. This closes only the import route: Claude Code's own tools can still read such a file when a teammate's words ask them to (end of this section). The seeded CLAUDE.md imports `@AGENTS.md` and `@INSTAFY.md` (packages/runtime-agent/assets/instafy/CLAUDE.md:1-2), which teammates can edit, so the walk must follow those imports too.
    - **Paths outside the folder.** Claude Code reads `~` as the home folder only before `/`, so the pre-flight reads any other `~…`, such as the version range in `zod@~3.22.4`, as a relative path like any other. Absolute paths and paths out of the folder have their `.` and `..` read as written, as Claude Code resolves a path. Of an absolute path, only the root's own entry is looked up, by the path's first name as written and in NFC, the form Claude Code gives every path it resolves. A path whose first name the root does not hold names nothing, so a path alias such as `@/components/ui/button` passes, and so does `@/` alone, while `@/etc/hosts` and `@/components/../etc/hosts` are refused. A path out of the folder that ends at a folder above it, such as `@..`, names a folder, which loads nothing; any other, such as `@../README.md`, is refused without a lookup. Nothing deeper outside the folder is looked up: every member can read a refusal's text through the message history (5.5), so a deeper lookup would tell them which paths exist on the person's computer, and looking up a path a teammate wrote can mount a network share (`/net/<host>`), raise a macOS privacy prompt or reach a per-process path such as `/dev/fd/N`, which runtime-agent sees otherwise than `claude` does.
    - **Instafy's own seeded files (D-B).** The untracked rule does not apply to the root `AGENTS.md` and `INSTAFY.md`. The Desktop scaffold writes them and commits them only into a new or seed `.instafy/.git`, so in a folder whose repository is the person's own `.git` they are untracked, and every turn would otherwise be refused. The exemption holds only when an import resolves to the very file the root holds under one of those names (compared as the folder compares names), a regular file and never a link, and never because of the name the import was written with. The pre-flight finds those files once, from the root's entries as they are when it starts. Every other rule still applies to them, and their own imports are walked with every rule, the untracked one included.
    - **A memory file that is a link** counts as an import of its target. It must resolve inside the folder to a tracked file or to one of those seeded root files; otherwise the turn is refused as `unsafe_import`, for example "AGENTS.md links to .env".
